Kinematically fixed correlators and associated conformal data for half-BPS operators.

Bulk-defect-defect correlators

Beyond the correlation functions discussed in the previous section, there are no further correlators that are completely fixed kinematically by conformal symmetry. It is still true, though, that conformal symmetry and supersymmetry strongly constrain the correlation functions that, once stripped of a prefactor encoding the transformation properties under the generators of the conformal group, can only depend on conformally invariant ratios of variables. Higher
n
-point correlation functions in the absence of any defect, for example, depend on
n(n-3)/2
spacetime cross-ratios, where
n<d+1
. For
n\geq d+1
the number is
nd-(d+1)(d+2)/2
, where the first term corresponds to the naive number of degrees of freedom, while the second term is the number of generators.
9
9

9

These correlators have been studied at weak and strong-coupling using a variety of techniques
[
169
,
168
,
66
]
.
The same is true for pure defect correlators, as they form a
1d
-CFT, with the remark that as
n\geq d+1
is always valid the number of conformally invariant variables is
n-3
.

The case of multipoint defect correlators –
m=0
in (
2.61
) – and the case of two-point functions in presence of the defect are the two most studied kind of correlators in the literature (see references
[
40
,
41
,
72
,
320
,
208
,
44
,
163
,
216
,
217
,
107
,
56
,
75
,
121
,
120
,
122
,
123
,
218
,
211
,
304
,
303
,
185
,
186
,
187
,
212
,
42
,
43
,
81
,
48
,
80
,
351
,
49
,
36
,
29
]
and the vast literature quoted therein). In this chapter, we focus on the largely overlooked bulk-defect-defect correlators having
m=1
and
n=2
and representing a particularly simple kinematically non-fixed correlator in the defect CFT. These correlators can be expressed as

\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle={\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}(\zeta;x)\,,

(2.79)

where
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}(\zeta;x)
is a reduced correlator, and
{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}
is a superconformal prefactor encoding the transformation properties of the correlator under conformal symmetry and
R
-symmetry. The conformal cross-ratios are defined as

x=\frac{|\vec{x}|_{1}^{2}\tau_{23}^{2}}{(\vec{x}_{1}^{2}+\tau_{12}^{2})(\vec{x}_{1}^{2}+\tau_{13}^{2})}\,,

(2.80)

for the spacetime variables, and

\zeta=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(u_{1}\cdot\theta)^{2}({\hat{u}}_{2}\cdot{\hat{u}}_{3})}{(u_{1}\cdot{\hat{u}}_{2})(u_{1}\cdot{\hat{u}}_{3})}\,.

(2.81)

The
-1/2
factor in our definition of
\zeta
is chosen such that the kinematic limit studied in
[
213
]
, which is known as the topological sector and will be introduced later in the chapter, corresponds to the sum of all the
R
symmetry channels defined below. The prefactor is chosen such that the leading order at weak coupling comes without a factor
\zeta
.
For instance, in the case
\Delta_{1}\geq{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
, this results in

{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}=\frac{(1\hat{2})^{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}(1\hat{3})^{{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}}{(1\theta)^{\Delta_{231}}}\,.

(2.82)

In our conventions, the reduced correlator is a polynomial in the
R
-symmetry variable
\zeta
.
Concretely,

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}(\zeta;x)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(\frac{\zeta}{x}\right)^{j-1}F_{j}(x)\,.

(2.83)

We call the functions
F_{j}

R
-symmetry channels. Each further power of
\zeta
corresponds to two fewer
u_{1}
vector contractions with the defect vectors
\hat{u}
and two more scalar interactions between the line and the bulk operator. The dependency on
x
in the prefactors is chosen such that they become
1
in the topological limit of
[
213
]
.
The number of channels
r=r(\Delta_{1},{\hat{\Delta}}_{2},{\hat{\Delta}}_{3})
for a specific configuration is given by

r=\left\lfloor\Delta_{1\hat{3}\hat{2}}\right\rfloor+1\,,

(2.84)

for
\Delta_{1}<{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
and

r={\hat{\Delta}}_{3}+1\,,

(2.85)

for
\Delta_{1}\geq{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
.
10
10

10

We always assume
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}\geq{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
without loss of generality.
. To clarify these formulas, consider an example having fixed values
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}=4
and
{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}=2
. For
\Delta_{1}=2
and
\Delta_{1}=3
the number of channels is only
1
. Then, for
\Delta_{1}=4,\,5
two
R
-symmetry channels are allowed. Finally, for
\Delta_{1}\geq 6
there are 3 different
R
-symmetry channels and their number stops growing.

The operator product expansion (OPE) can be used to expand the correlator (
2.79
) into conformal blocks. One can either start with an OPE of the two defect operators
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}\times\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}
, or with the OPE of the bulk operator with the line defect,
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}\times\mathcal{W}_{\ell}
.
These two operations lead to the same expression, leading to the conformal block expansion

F_{j}(x)=x^{-\Delta_{1}}\sum_{{\hat{\Delta}}\>\mathrm{prim.}}b_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(j)}\hat{\lambda}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(j)}g_{{\hat{\Delta}}}(x)\,,

(2.86)

where the sum runs over all the primaries, including superdescendants. Because of the equivalence of the OPE expansions, there exist no crossing relations connecting different expansions of the same correlators. Although not specified explicitly here to avoid cluttering the notation, the blocks
g_{{\hat{\Delta}}}(x)
depend on the scaling dimensions
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}
and
{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
(but not
\Delta_{1}
).
They have been determined in
[
112
,
338
]
to be

g_{{\hat{\Delta}}}(x)=x^{{\hat{\Delta}}/2}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(({\hat{\Delta}}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}-{\hat{\Delta}}_{3})/2,({\hat{\Delta}}-{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3})/2;{\hat{\Delta}}+1/2;x\right)\,.

(2.87)

\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}
=\sum\limits_{{\hat{\Delta}}}\hat{\lambda}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}{\hat{\Delta}}}
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}

Figure 2.3
:
Illustration of the operator product expansion for the bulk-defect-defect correlators.
The result is an expansion in conformal blocks, with the OPE coefficients being given by the product of defect three-point and bulk-defect two-point coefficients.

The OPE is illustrated in Figure
2.3
. Representations of the correlators different from the sum (
2.86
) can also be obtained: in particular, it is possible to express the sum as over superprimaries instead of primaries, by determining the superconformal blocks. Further in the chapter we provide the superblock expansion for the case
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
. An alternative expansion of bulk-defect-defect was derived in
[
301
,
302
]
by using locality properties of the correlator: this block expansion is known as the local block expansion.

In the rest of the chapter, we will focus on the computation of bulk-defect-defect correlators using a mix of non-perturbative and perturbative techniques (mostly for a small ’t Hooft coupling (
2.36
)). Before presenting the results up to NLO in the perturbative expansion, we introduce the non-perturbative constraints on the correlators which we use to reduce the perturbative information needed to complete the calculation.

2.3
Non-perturbative constraints

We start the presentation of non-perturbative constraints by showing how superconformal symmetry can be used to trade a function of the spacetime cross-ratio for a constant.
This constant, called the topological sector, can be evaluated using localization techniques
[
349
,
348
,
363
,
213
]
.
We then present the superconformal block expansion – which will be used later in section
2.4.1
to study the case
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
at strong coupling.
Finally, we briefly discuss locality properties of the block expansion introduced in
[
301
,
302
]
and pinching and splitting limits, which relate parts of the bulk-defect-defect correlators to lower-point functions.

2.3.1
Topological sector

Superconformal symmetry can typically be encoded in the form of superconformal Ward identities (SCWI).
They are powerful tools for constraining correlators of half-BPS operators, both in
{\mathcal{N}}=4
sYM
[
154
,
157
,
158
]
and in the Wilson-line defect CFT
[
304
,
303
,
42
,
81
,
48
,
29
]
.
For bulk-defect-defect correlators, SCWIs are currently not known and a rigorous derivation has yet to be done. The attempt of SCWI guessing done in
[
30
]
has led to a differential constraint that is fully encoded by the existence of the
topological sector
.

Our starting point is the topological sector calculated in
[
213
]
. This sector corresponds to a specific
R
-symmetry polarization of a bulk-defect-defect correlator,
i.e.
a specific choice of vectors
u_{1}
,
{\hat{u}}_{2}
, and
{\hat{u}}_{3}
such that
\zeta=x
. The powerful idea explored in
[
213
]
is the correspondence of the correlators in this subsector with the bosonic two-dimensional Yang-
Mills theory on
S^{2}
. The computation in the two-dimensional Yang-Mills
theory can then be further reduced to simple Gaussian (multi-)matrix models
[
215
,
349
,
212
]
, and the
results depend only on the area of the region inside the Wilson loop. The topological sector of bulk-defect-defect correlators is related to a small deformation of the Wilson loop and can be computed by acting with differential operators on one-point correlators in the presence of the defect. The result has no kinematic dependence; in our conventions, the topological sector can be expressed as the sum of the
R
-symmetry channels of (
2.83
):

\sum_{j=1}^{r}F_{j}(x)={\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}\,.

(2.88)

The right-hand side
{\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}
is a constant, in the sense that it depends neither on spacetime nor
R
-symmetry variables.
It is however a function of the coupling
\lambda
, exactly as scaling dimensions and three-operator interaction constants
\lambda_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}}
.
Equation (
2.88
) can immediately be used to eliminate one
R
-symmetry channel in (
2.83
):

F_{r}(x)={\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}-\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}F_{j}(x)\,.

(2.89)

In fact, in
[
30
]
, we formulated an Ansatz for the Ward identities based on known examples
[
304
,
303
,
42
,
81
,
48
,
29
]
and used the results of the correlators up to NLO at weak coupling to constrain the numerical coefficients.
We found the differential constraint

\left.\bigl(\partial_{x}+\partial_{\zeta}\bigr){\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}(\zeta;x)\right|_{\zeta=x}=0\,.

(2.90)

Unfortunately, this equation can be shown not to encode more constraints than (
2.88
).
It is possible that the fact that the correlator depends on only one spacetime and one
R
-symmetry cross-ratio does not leave much room for supersymmetry to constrain its form any further.
It is also possible that differential constraints not taking the form (
2.90
) are being missed by this strategy.
For instance, in five-point functions of half-BPS operators in
{\mathcal{N}}=4
sYM, this Ansatz approach proved unsuccessful, although it is known that differential constraints exist
[
319
]
.
For the time being, we use the constraint (
2.89
) to study the correlators at leading and next-to-leading orders at weak coupling in the rest of the chapter. Although we know that the value of the topological sector (
2.88
) only depends on the ’t Hooft coupling, to give precise results for the correlators it is important to fix the value of this constant to give correct results for bulk-defect-defect correlators. The next section deals with the expansion of localization results from
[
213
]
up to NLO at weak coupling.

Localization results

The topological sector can be evaluated for arbitrary
\Delta_{1}
,
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}
,
{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
using the localization techniques of
[
213
]
. The derivatives with respect to the area of the Wilson-loop of the one-point bulk correlation functions lead to angular integrals of rational functions of trigonometric functions. Consider for example the case
\Delta_{1}\geq{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
: the arguments in
[
213
]
express the localization result as

\mathbb{G}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}=(-ig)^{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}2^{-\Delta_{1}/2}\frac{\sqrt{\Delta_{1}}}{N}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\mu B(\Delta_{1})U_{C}({\hat{\Delta}}_{2},\cos\theta)U_{C}({\hat{\Delta}}_{3},\cos\theta)

(2.91)

where we used a different letter as this result is derived for a circular Wilson line and under a different normalization
11
11

11

Note that this only results in the need to multiply the localization result from
[
213
]
by a correct normalization factor to get the results reported in the following section.
. The functions
U_{C}({\hat{\Delta}},x)
are Chebyshev polynomials in the variable
x
and the measure
d\mu B(\Delta_{1})
can be written as

d\mu B(\Delta_{1})=\frac{e^{i\Delta_{1}\theta}}{2\pi i^{\Delta_{1}}}\exp(4\pi g\sin\theta)d\theta

(2.92)

In principle, the localization result is exact and the exponential in (
2.92
) can be expanded to any order in perturbation theory; in practice, the integrals appearing may become complicated and prevent an analitical expression
12
12

12

To be completely precise, we should mention that for higher orders in perturbation theory the product of Chebyshev polynomials becomes the product of two sums of Chebyshev polynomials.
. Here we expand the localization results to next-to-leading order, obtaining explicit formulas for all cases.

Figure 2.4
:
The figure displays the topological sector for
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
.
The blue line is the exact expression, presented in (
2.114
).
The green and orange lines correspond, respectively, to the weak- and strong-coupling expansions up to next-to-leading order (weak coupling) and next-to-next-to-leading order (strong coupling), derived in
[
213
]
and reproduced here in (
2.94
), (
2.96
), and (
2.97
).

Weak coupling.

At weak coupling, the topological sector follows the perturbative structure

{\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}=\frac{\lambda^{a/2}}{N}\biggl({\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}^{(0)}+\lambda{\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}^{(1)}+\ldots\biggr)\,,

(2.93)

where
a=\text{min}(a_{4})
is introduced in (
2.71
) and corresponds to the minimum number of propagators connecting the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}
and the Wilson line. For a given configuration
\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle
, the leading order is given by

\begin{split}{\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}^{(0)}&=\frac{2^{\frac{1}{2}(2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}-2)}i^{a-2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}}\sqrt{\Delta_{1}}}{\Gamma(a+1)}\left((2-2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1})\Theta(1-a)+2^{1-a}\Theta(a)\right)\,,\end{split}

(2.94)

where we use the following conventions for the Heaviside step function:

\Theta(x)=\begin{cases}0\,,&\text{ if }x\leq 0\,,\\
+1\,,&\text{ if }x>0\,.\end{cases}

(2.95)

This formula is the compact version of the results presented in Appendix B.1 of
[
213
]
.

The next-to-leading order contribution to
{\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}
is 0 for
\Delta_{1}\leq{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}-{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
and
\Delta_{1}<{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
with
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}-\Delta_{1}
odd. For the other cases (
\Delta_{1}\geq{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
or
\Delta_{1}<{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
with
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}-\Delta_{1}
even), we find

\begin{split}{\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}^{(1)}=&-\frac{2^{-1+3\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}}\sqrt{\Delta_{1}}\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}\lambda^{a/2}}{N}\bigg[\frac{(-1)^{\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}}}{2^{2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}}\Gamma(4+a)}\Theta(\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1})\\
&+\frac{1}{\Gamma(3-2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1})}\left(1-\Theta(\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1})\right)\bigg]-\frac{1}{12}{\mathbb{F}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}^{(0)}\,,\end{split}

(2.96)

where the Heaviside step function is defined as in (
2.95
).

Strong coupling.

The strong-coupling limit of the topological sector can be evaluated using the already mentioned tools of
[
213
]
for large ’t Hooft coupling.
We focus here only on the case
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
, since it is the only one that we consider at strong coupling.
It reads

{\mathbb{F}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}=\frac{1}{N}\biggl(-\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{\sqrt{2}}+\frac{9}{2\sqrt{2}}-\frac{15}{8\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}+...\biggr)+\ldots\,.

(2.97)

In the above formula, the expansion within the brackets is in powers of
1/\sqrt{\lambda}
while the dots outside of the brackets represent the expansion in
1/N
.
The topological sector at weak and strong coupling is plotted in Figure
2.4
for
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
, together with its exact expression introduced in the next section.

2.3.2
Superblock expansion

The constraints from superconformal symmetry discussed above can be used to derive an expansion in superconformal blocks for the bulk-defect-defect correlators.
Generically, the expansion in superblocks re-sums the contributions of the conformal primaries that are not superconformal in the conformal block expansion (
2.86
). The superconformal block expansion gives the expansion for the full correlator (as opposed to the conformal block expansion that is valid for each
R
-symmetry channel separately) and it takes the form

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}(\zeta;x)=\sum_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}b_{\Delta_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}}\hat{\lambda}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}(\zeta;x)\,,

(2.98)

where
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
designates the superprimary operators described in section
2.1.3
appearing in the OPE
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}\times\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}
.
For the case of the Wilson-line CFT, this OPE is given, e.g., in
[
186
]
and reads

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}\times\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}\longrightarrow\sum_{m={\hat{\Delta}}_{23}\text{ step }2}^{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}{\mathcal{B}}_{m}+\sum_{i=0}^{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{i}\sum_{{\hat{\Delta}}^{(0)}>2i+{\hat{\Delta}}_{23}+1}{\mathcal{L}}_{[2i-2j,2j+{\hat{\Delta}}_{23}],0}^{{\hat{\Delta}}}\,.

(2.99)

In the case
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}={\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
, the leading contribution corresponds to the identity through the identification
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}=\hat{\mathbf{1}}
.
The operators
{\mathcal{B}}_{m}
are half-BPS and therefore protected from corrections to their scaling dimensions, while the so-called
long operators

{\mathcal{L}}_{[a,b],0}^{{\hat{\Delta}}}
are unprotected and have a non-vanishing anomalous dimension.
The lowest-lying non-protected operator (both at weak and strong coupling) is
\phi^{6}
, which has dimension
[
13
,
227
,
121
]

\displaystyle\Delta_{\phi^{6}}

\displaystyle\overset{\lambda\sim 0}{=}1+\frac{\lambda}{4\pi^{2}}+\ldots\,,

(2.100)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\phi^{6}}

\displaystyle\overset{\lambda\gg 1}{=}2-\frac{5}{\sqrt{\lambda}}+\ldots\,.

(2.101)

In this section, we will focus on the bulk-defect-defect correlator of the lowest dimensional protected operators:
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
. For this correlator, the content of the OPE is

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\times\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\longrightarrow\hat{\mathbf{1}}+{\mathcal{B}}_{2}+\sum_{{\hat{\Delta}}^{0}\geq 1}{\mathcal{L}}_{[0,0],0}^{\hat{\Delta}}\,.

(2.102)

We now discuss how to derive the explicit form of the superblock expansion presented in
[
30
]
13
13

13

We thank Gabriel Bliard and Philine van Vliet for sharing their preliminary results for these superblocks and useful conversations around this topic.
. First of all, it is necessary to remark that in a perturbative setting, operators can become degenerate
[
303
,
291
]
.
The degeneracy grows with the tree-level scaling dimension
\Delta^{(0)}
.
The large
N
limit helps to reduce the degeneracy, but there are still many operators with the same tree-level scaling dimensions.
14
14

14

A counting of the number of operators can be found in
[
186
]
.

From (
2.102
), we can read the expansion of
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
in superblocks:

{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}(\zeta;x)=a_{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathbf{1}}}(\zeta;x)+b_{2\hat{2}}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}\hat{2}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{2}}(\zeta;x)+\sum_{{\hat{\Delta}}}b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}(\zeta;x)\,,

(2.103)

where
{\hat{\Delta}}
in the last sum refers to the operators of
{\mathcal{L}}_{[0,0],0}^{{\hat{\Delta}}}
.

Each superblock can be decomposed into an
R
-symmetry and a spacetime part through the following sum over the Dynkin labels of the
R
-symmetry and the scaling dimensions, here labelled as
r
:

{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}(\zeta;x)=\sum_{a,b,r}\alpha_{[a,b],r}h_{[a,b]}(\zeta)g_{r}(x)\,,

(2.104)

where
g_{{\hat{\Delta}}}(x)
refers to the bosonic blocks already introduced in (
2.87
).
The range of the sums is determined by considering the content of the supermultiplets.
This analysis was done in
[
186
]
for the case of the Wilson-line defect CFT.
We then apply the superconformal constraints (
2.90
) on the blocks (or equivalently the topological sector constraint (
2.89
) to the correlator) to fix the open coefficients
\alpha_{[a,b],r}
.
This amounts to requiring that individual blocks satisfy the symmetries of the correlator.

The identity block is simply given by

{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathbf{1}}}(\zeta;x)=-\frac{2\zeta}{x}\,,

(2.105)

where the factor
2
is there to account for the one present in the definition (
2.81
).

For the operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
, we see in (B.4) of
[
186
]
that the block should take the form

{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{2}}(\zeta;x)=\alpha_{[0,2],2}h_{[0,2]}(\zeta)x^{-1}g_{2}(x)+\alpha_{[2,0],3}h_{[2,0]}(\zeta)x^{-1}g_{3}(x)+\alpha_{[0,0],4}h_{[0,0]}(\zeta)x^{-1}g_{4}(x)\,.

(2.106)

Note that we have selected scalar contributions in the supermultiplet.
Generically,
R
-symmetry blocks depend on the external operators, and for
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
they are expected to take the form

h_{[a,b]}(\zeta)=\beta_{[a,b]}^{(0)}+\beta_{[a,b]}^{(1)}\zeta\,,

(2.107)

where the coefficients
\beta_{[a,b]}^{(0)}
are so far unfixed.
Applying the SCWI (
2.90
) and choosing the normalization according to the highest-weight channel, we finally obtain

{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{2}}(\zeta;x)=\biggl(1-\frac{2}{5}\zeta\biggr)x^{-1}g_{2}(x)-\frac{12}{175}\frac{\zeta}{x}g_{4}(x)\,,

(2.108)

The same method can be applied to the superblocks of the long operators, yielding

\begin{split}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}(\zeta;x)&=-\frac{\zeta}{x}g_{\hat{\Delta}}(x)+\biggl(1-\frac{2\left(\Delta^{2}+3\Delta+1\right)\zeta}{4\Delta^{2}+12\Delta+5}\biggr)x^{-1}g_{{\hat{\Delta}}+2}(x)\\
&\phantom{=\ }-\frac{(\Delta+2)^{2}(\Delta+3)^{2}}{(2\Delta+3)(2\Delta+5)^{2}(2\Delta+7)}\frac{\zeta}{x}g_{{\hat{\Delta}}+4}(x)\,.\end{split}

(2.109)

The expansion in superblocks can be expanded in the coupling constant, through

\Delta=\Delta^{(0)}+\lambda\gamma^{(1)}_{\Delta^{(0)}}+\ldots\,,

(2.110)

and similarly for OPE coefficients.
This allows a perturbative analysis, which will play an important role further in the chapter.
It is important however to notice that, as mentioned above, OPE coefficients and scaling dimensions become degenerate in a perturbative setting.
Instead of individual coefficients, we are therefore forced to consider their
average
only.
For instance,

\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(\ell)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(\ell)}\,\rangle=\sum_{\text{deg}}b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(\ell)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(\ell)}\,,

(2.111)

where the sum is over all the operators that have the same tree-level scaling dimension
\Delta^{(0)}
.
Note that we are using here the notation of
[
186
]
.
Moreover, the same analysis can be performed at strong coupling, and it is known that in this regime the degeneracy is lifted slower than at weak coupling
[
303
,
185
]
.

The superblock expansion can be studied in the topological sector as well.
It is well-known that only half-BPS contributions survive in this kinematic limit
[
213
]
.
For the case of
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
, we have

{\mathbb{F}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}=-2a_{2}+\langle\,b_{2\hat{2}}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}\hat{2}}\,\rangle\,.

(2.112)

This provides an exact formula for the topological sector discussed in Section
2.3.1
.
The average above corresponds to the contributions from the two operators
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
and
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(0|2)}
– an operator defined from multi-trace operators via a Gram-Schmidt procedure
[
213
]
to obtain orthogonal operators to the single trace ones
15
15

15

In reference
[
30
]
we define the non orthogonal multi-trace operators employed in this procedure.
.
This degeneracy is in fact absent, as it can be seen in the following way.
From the Gram-Schmidt procedure mentioned above, the good operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(0|2)}
takes the form

\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(0|2)}\sim\bigl(\hat{\mathrm{O}}_{(0|2)}-\langle\,\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\hat{\mathrm{O}}_{(0|2)}\,\rangle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\bigr)\,.

(2.113)

The corresponding OPE coefficient
b_{2(0|2)}
is of order
{\mathcal{O}}(N^{0})
.
However the three-point function
\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}(0|\hat{2})}
vanishes as a consequence of (
2.113
).
The topological sector is therefore simply given by

{\mathbb{F}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}=-2a_{2}+b_{2\hat{2}}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}\hat{2}}\,.

(2.114)

This expression is plotted in figure
2.4
from weak to strong coupling, using the analytic results (
2.69
), (
2.78
) and (
2.73
), and is shown to agree well with the perturbative results of
[
213
]
.

2.3.3
Pinching and splitting

In this section, we present two kinematic limits corresponding to the reduction of some bulk-defect-defect correlators to kinematically fixed functions, in particular to two-point bulk-defect functions (pinching limit) and products of one-point bulk correlators and two-point defect correlators (splitting limit). In our pre-turbative bootstrap framework introduced in
[
30
,
29
]
, lower-point functions serve as further non-perturbative information constraining the correlators under study and can serve to fix overall constraints in the
R
-symmetry channels.

Pinching limit

The pinching of the two defect operators into one having scaling dimension being the sum of the original two, i.e., the limit of the correlator
\tau_{3}\rightarrow\tau_{2}
and
{\hat{u}}_{3}\rightarrow{\hat{u}}_{2}
, results in the OPE coefficient
b_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}
after accounting for the different normalization constants:

\displaystyle\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}\,\rangle=\frac{\sqrt{{\hat{n}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}{\hat{n}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}}}{\sqrt{{\hat{n}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}}}\lim\limits_{3\to 2}\,\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle=b_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}\,(12)^{{\hat{\Delta}}}(1\theta)^{\Delta_{1}-{\hat{\Delta}}}\,,

(2.115)

with
{\hat{\Delta}}={\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
.
This provides a valuable limit of the bulk-defect-defect correlator, since the coefficients
b_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}
can be evaluated (see (
2.76
)). For this limit to be possible, it is necessary that at least one channel without contractions
(\hat{2}\hat{3})
is present, as it becomes zero in this limit. This condition corresponds to
\Delta_{1}\geq{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
.
If one inserts the expansion in channels (
2.83
) in (
2.115
), only the channel
F_{1}
computed at
x=0
survives, as
\zeta=0

\displaystyle\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=(12)^{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}(1\theta)^{-2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}}\biggl(F_{1}^{(0)}(0)+\lambda\biggl(F_{1}^{(1)}(0)-\frac{F_{1}^{(0))}(0)}{48}\biggr)+{\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{2})\biggr)\,,

(2.116)

from which it is possible to extract the pinching constraints at leading and next-to-leading orders:

\displaystyle F_{1}^{(0)}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\Delta_{1}}}{(-2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1})!}\frac{\lambda^{-\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}}}{2^{-3\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}}N}\,,

(2.117)

\displaystyle F_{1}^{(1)}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\Delta_{1}}}{(-2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1})!}\frac{\lambda^{-\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}}}{2^{(-3\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1}+2)}N}\frac{(1+2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1})}{3(2-2\Delta_{\hat{2}\hat{3}1})}\,.

(2.118)

It might be tempting to consider another pinching limit, which consists of bringing the bulk operator in a third position on the line in order to generate a double-trace defect operator.
This operator would not be orthonormal to the single-trace operators and will be discussed later in the chapter.

Splitting limit

A different limit comes from considering a large separation between the bulk operator and the defect ones, i.e.,
|\vec{x}_{1}|\to\infty
.
In the correlator expressed in
R-
symmetry channels (
2.83
), this corresponds to the limit
x\rightarrow 0
of the whole correlator without setting
\zeta=0
(which would correspond to the previous pinching as mentioned above).
In this case, the bulk-defect-defect correlator factorizes into a product of a two-point defect correlator and a one-point function of a bulk operator. Note that since the operators do not change, there is no role played by the normalization factors. The correlator thus becomes

\lim\limits_{\chi\to 0}\,\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle=\langle\,\Delta_{1}\,\rangle\langle\,{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle\,,

(2.119)

from which (
2.83
) simplifies to

\lim\limits_{x\to 0}\,\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle=a_{\Delta_{1}}{\mathcal{K}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}\left(-2\frac{\zeta}{x}\right)^{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}\delta_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}\,,

(2.120)

where the coefficient
a_{\Delta_{1}}
is given in (
2.69
). Note that the factor
(-2)
in (
2.120
) is there because of the definition we have chosen of the
R
-symmetry cross ratio (
2.81
), which simplifies the formulation of the topological sector.
The splitting limit can serve as a constraint or consistency check for the case
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}={\hat{\Delta}}_{3}
: Equation (
2.120
) combined with (
2.83
) gives

F_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+1}(0)=\left(-2\right)^{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}a_{\Delta_{1}}\delta_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}\,,

(2.121)

which is valid non-perturbatively. Note that (
2.121
) is consistent with the non-perturbative result for the topological sector of
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
in (
2.112
).

2.3.4
Locality

In this section, we review the principles behind the local block expansion introduced for bulk-defect-defect correlators in
[
301
,
302
]
. We decide to keep this analysis at the level of the superblock expansion, but we stress that the original paper does not consider supersymmetry: here we simply mean that we apply the analysis to all
R
-symmetry channels, potentially considering them separately. We begin by considering the analyticity property of the correlator of not having a discontinuity for
z\geq 1
16
16

16

We define the discontinuity operator
\mathcal{I}_{z}
acting on
F(z)
as
\mathcal{I}_{z}F(z)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(F(z+i\epsilon)-F(z-i\epsilon)\right)/2i
, as this point does not correspond to any of the operators crossing each other positions.

\mathcal{I}_{z}{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}=0\qquad\text{for $z\geq 1$}

(2.122)

However, at this point the conformal blocks appearing in the (super)-conformal block expansion (
2.103
) are discontinuous – as all conformal blocks involved for any dimensions of the external operators
[
112
,
338
]
are. By pluggin the expansion (
2.103
) in (
2.122
) we get the following infinite sum

\mathcal{I}_{z}\left(\sum_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}b_{\Delta_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}}\hat{\lambda}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}(\zeta;x)\right)=0

(2.123)

running over the superconformal primaries. Given that the sum is an infinite sum, it is not justified to swap the sum and the discontinuity operator
17
17

17

When the sum runs over a finite number of fields we can consider the sum of the discontinuities of the blocks.
In order to de-clutter the notation, we redefine the coefficients of the superblocks as

c_{\hat{\Delta}}=b_{\Delta_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}}\hat{\lambda}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}\hat{\mathcal{O}}}

(2.124)

where, for practical applications in perturbative frameworks, degenerate operators will be considered together in the same coefficient. To make use of the condition on the OPE coefficients expressed in the sum in (
2.123
), we want to write a dispersion relation for the correlator in the analysis. Given that we are interested in manipulating the contour around
z=0
– where the correlator branch cut for
z<0
starts – we consider a modified correlator

\bar{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}-\sum_{0<{\hat{\Delta}}<2\tilde{\alpha}}c_{\hat{\Delta}}{\mathcal{L}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}(\zeta;x)

(2.125)

where

{\mathcal{L}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}(\zeta;x)={\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}(\zeta;x)+O(z^{\tilde{\alpha}+\epsilon})\,.

(2.126)

Figure 2.5
:
The figure displays the analytic structure of the superblock
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}=2}(\zeta;x)
. We see that they have a discontinuity at
x\geq 1
, which is incompatible with the locality requirement discussed here. Note that the superblocks
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}>2}(\zeta;x)
exhibit the same behavior.

The Cauchy formula

\bar{{\mathcal{A}}}(\zeta;w)=\oint\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\left(\frac{w}{z}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}+1}\frac{\bar{{\mathcal{A}}}(\zeta;z)}{z-w}

(2.127)

can then be manipulated to become a dispersion relation for the modified correlator,
i.e.
an integral involving its discontinuity, which is non-zero only for
z<0
.

\bar{{\mathcal{A}}}(\zeta;w)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{dz}{\pi}\left(\frac{w}{z}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}+1}\frac{{\mathcal{I}}_{z}\bar{{\mathcal{A}}}(\zeta;z)}{z-w}

(2.128)

we can then define the local blocks as the same dispersion formulas for the superblocks and then find

{\mathcal{L}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}(\zeta;w)={\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}(\zeta;w)-\int_{1}^{+\infty}\frac{dz}{\pi}\left(\frac{w}{z}\right)^{\tilde{\alpha}+1}\frac{{\mathcal{I}}_{z}{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}(\zeta;z)}{z-w}\,,

(2.129)

which then gives a dispersion formula for the bulk-defect-defect correlator which can be proven
[
301
,
302
]
to be equivalent to the condition

\sum_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}c_{\hat{\Delta}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}(\zeta;x)=\sum_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}c_{\hat{\Delta}}{\mathcal{L}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}(\zeta;w)\,.

(2.130)

This result is particularly interesting because, as we have mentioned above, there is no crossing relation for bulk-defect-defect correlators as both OPE limits give the same expression. Locality conditions give a similar constraint on the correlator with respect to crossing, and the condition translates into a sum rule for the OPE coefficients

\sum_{\hat{\Delta}}c_{\hat{\Delta}}\theta_{n}^{\tilde{\alpha}}\left({\hat{\Delta}}\right)=0

(2.131)

where the functions
\theta_{n}^{\tilde{\alpha}}\left({\hat{\Delta}}\right)
are given in equation (3.27) of
[
301
]
. Despite being a relevant non-perturbative constraint for bulk-defect-defect correlators, locality at the moment does not play a role in our perturbative bootstrap approach; however, they represent a relevant consistency check and can be employed in the future for the determination of strong-coupling correlators at NNLO (see section
2.4.1
).

2.4
Perturbative results

In this part of the chapter, we present explicit results for the bulk-defect-defect correlators introduced in section
2.2.3
, and for which we listed non-perturbative constraints in the previous section
2.3
.
We begin by determining the contributions to the correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
up to next-to-leading order at weak and strong coupling, and we extract the corresponding CFT data using the superblocks presented in Section
2.3.2
.
At strong coupling, we also present partial results for the next-to-next-to-leading order, for which one rational function remains unfixed but is shown to be strongly constrained by the collection of sum rules
2.131
. We then generalize our results by computing all correlators up to next-to-leading order at weak coupling. We observe that transcendental terms are systematically absent at this order, which results in relations for the OPE coefficients.

2.4.1
The correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle

The correlation function between one protected bulk operator of dimension two and two protected defect operators of dimension one represents the easiest example of bulk-defect-defect correlator, since the trace in the definition of the half-BPS bulk operators (
2.33
) implies that the dimension one bulk operator of this kind is trivially zero. Because of its simplicity, the correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
is the ideal case to test how the combination of non-perturbative and perturbative information can determine bulk-defect-defect correlators at weak coupling. Many of the properties of this initial correlator are indeed maintained in correlators of operators of higher scaling dimension: let us then start the analysis of
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
by establishing the notation and examining the leading order contribution.

Weak coupling

Perturbative structure

At weak coupling, the bulk-defect-defect correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
has the following perturbative structure at large
N
:

{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}(\zeta;x)=\frac{1}{N}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(0)}(\zeta;x)+\lambda\,{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(1)}(\zeta;x)+\ldots\bigr)+\ldots\,,

(2.132)

where as usual the
\ldots
inside the brackets refer to higher powers of
\lambda
, while the
\ldots
at the end of the expression refer to corrections in
N
.
As explained in (
2.83
), the correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
consists of
two

R
-symmetry channels:

{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(\ell)}(\zeta;x)=F_{1}^{(\ell)}(x)+\frac{\zeta}{x}F_{2}^{(\ell)}(x)\,.

(2.133)

From the number of
R
-symmetry channels and the solution to the topological constraint (
2.89
) it immediately follows that one channel is sufficient to completely determine the whole correlator. This makes the case in analysis ideal to test the validity of our approach by computing both channels up to NLO and testing how the perturbative bootstrap approach can simplify the computation of bulk-defect-defect correlators compared to a pure perturbative approach.

Leading order

We first determine the leading order for the correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
.
At this order, the correlator consists of a single Feynman diagram, which can be represented as

{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(0)}(\zeta;x)=\hbox to33.3pt{\vbox to36.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\lower-2.33386pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 43.31 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 16.54 0 L 16.54 39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,

(2.134)

and only consists of the free propagators defined in (
2.39
).
It is easy to see that the
R
-symmetry channels take the form

F_{1}^{(0)}(x)=c_{0}\,,\qquad F_{2}^{(0)}(x)=0\,,

(2.135)

where
c_{0}
is a constant that can be fixed either through direct calculation, from the pinching constraint of (
2.117
), or the topological sector (
2.94
).
At this order, we find

c_{0}=b_{2\hat{2}}^{(0)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}\hat{2}}^{(0)}=\sqrt{2}\,.

(2.136)

Note that the identity operator in the OPE (
2.102
) does not contribute to the superconformal block expansion at this order, i.e., we have

a_{2}^{(0)}=0\,.

(2.137)

It is a trivial task to extract the CFT data for these results using the superblock expansion (
2.103
).
For the long operators labelled
{\mathcal{L}}_{[0,0],0}^{\hat{\Delta}}

[
303
]
, we find the following closed form:

\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(0)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(0)}\,\rangle=\begin{cases}0\,,&\text{ if }{\hat{\Delta}}^{(0)}\text{ odd}\,,\\
\frac{(-1)^{{\hat{\Delta}}/2}\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma({\hat{\Delta}}+2)}{2^{{\hat{\Delta}}+1/2}\Gamma({\hat{\Delta}}+3/2)}\,,&\text{ if }{\hat{\Delta}}^{(0)}\text{ even}\,.\end{cases}

(2.138)

This is the supersymmetric version of the formula given in
[
112
,
338
,
301
,
302
]
, which corresponds to an expansion in bosonic blocks. We remark that in any perturbative expansion, operators become degenerate and therefore the CFT data are obtained as averages over operators having the same classical scaling dimension.

Next-to-leading order

Table 2.3
:
Feynman diagrams contributing to the correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
at next-to-leading order.

F_{1}^{(1)}(x)

F_{2}^{(1)}(x)

At NLO, the Feynman diagrams corresponding to the correlator are listed in Table
2.3
.
Note that each open line in (
2.142
)-(
2.143
) can end on the orange dots placed along the defect. Due to the operator normalization ((
2.55
) and following), it is important to remember that a contribution from the lower order diagram is also present, multiplied by the NLO expansion of the normalization factor. Thanks to the non-perturbative constraints, we do not have to calculate all the diagrams in table
2.3
.
In particular, we can focus on the second
R
-symmetry channel and therefore on the diagrams that do not contain bulk vertices. The integrals necessary to compute this channel arise from the coupling of
\phi^{6}
with the Wilson line and are

I(a)=\int_{-\infty}^{a}d\tau_{1}I_{1\tau_{1}}...\int_{-\infty}^{\tau_{n-1}}d\tau_{\Delta_{1}-({\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3})}I_{1\tau_{\Delta_{1}-({\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3})}}=\frac{1}{n!}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{a}d\tau I_{1\tau}\right)^{n}\,,

(2.139)

and similar from a point
a
to
+\infty
.In this case, the number of nested integrals
n
is two.
Thanks to their relation to the one-dimensional integral of a single propagator, the integrals of the kind (
2.139
) are easy to compute using the results

\int_{-\infty}^{a}d\tau I_{1\tau}=\frac{\pi+2\arctan\left(\frac{a}{|x_{\perp}|}\right)}{2|x_{\perp}|}\,,

(2.140)

and

\int_{a}^{+\infty}d\tau I_{1\tau}=\frac{\pi-2\arctan\left(\frac{a}{|x_{\perp}|}\right)}{2|x_{\perp}|}\,.

(2.141)

From the relations listed above, we obtain the results for the two diagrams contributing to the channel we focus on

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}\frac{1}{16\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{x}\biggl(\pi+2\text{arctan}\biggl(\sqrt{\frac{1-x}{x}}\biggr)\biggr)^{2}\,,

(2.142)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}\frac{1}{16\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{x}\biggl(\pi-2\text{arctan}\biggl(\sqrt{\frac{1-x}{x}}\biggr)\biggr)^{2}\,,

(2.143)

where the prefactors result from symmetry factors and trace contributions. Putting the two diagrams together, we obtain for the
R
-symmetry channel
F_{2}
the result

\displaystyle F_{2}^{(1)}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}}(\pi^{2}+4\text{arccos}^{2}\sqrt{x})\,.

(2.144)

As a consistency test, we can check that this expression is compatible with the splitting constraint of Section
2.3.3
:

F_{2}^{(1)}(0)=-2a_{2}^{(1)}=-\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}\,.

(2.145)

The channel
F_{1}
can now be obtained through the supersymmetry constraints of Section
2.3.1
.
The solution (
2.89
) to the Ward identity implies

F_{1}^{(1)}(x)={\mathbb{F}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(1)}-F_{2}^{(1)}(x)\,.

(2.146)

Using the results of
[
213
]
(summarized in Section
2.3.1
), we can calculate the topological sector, and we get

{\mathbb{F}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(1)}=-\frac{1}{6\sqrt{2}}\,.

(2.147)

The correlator is now completely fixed.
Notice that this result was obtained without considering the first line of Table
2.3
, where the Feynman diagrams consist of bulk vertices and are significantly harder than the first line. This strategy will allow us in the next sections to provide results for any correlator up to NLO in the weak coupling perturbative expansion. We decide therefore to show the validity and the advantages of this approach by explicitly computing the diagrams contributing to the channel
F_{1}^{(1)}(x)
. The results are obtained using the Feynman rules of Section
2.2.1
and
2.2.2
and the integrals of Appendix
A
, where the regularization for UV divergent integrals is also described.

We begin with the self-energy diagrams.

2.44
), it is easy to find that

(2.149)

Note that these results are given in the conformal limit
\tau_{3}\to\infty
.
In other words, each diagram contains corrections in
1/\tau_{3}
and divergences proportional to
\log\tau_{3}
, however since we know that the correlator depends on a single cross-ratio
x
, they are expected to cancel once added up. As expected from the final result (
2.146
), we will indeed observe a cancellation of the
\log\tau_{3}
terms when summing all diagrams contributing to the
R
-symmetry channel
F_{1}^{(1)}(x)
. Next, we consider the two diagrams that involve bulk vertices but no integration along the Wilson line.
The first one contains the X-integral defined in appendix
A
and pinched to.

(2.151)

Finally, two diagrams involve an integral along the Wilson line, as well as a bulk vertex.
These integrals are more challenging but can be performed analytically in the conformal frame
\tau_{3}\to\infty
. For example, the integrals can be manipulated to extract their divergences and then evaluated numerically to be matched to an ansatz made of the expected functions appearing in the result.

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \color[rgb]{0.9492,0.5742,0.1133}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 37.4 0 M 40.16 0 C 40.16 1.52 38.92 2.76 37.4 2.76 C 35.88 2.76 34.65 1.52 34.65 0 C 34.65 -1.52 35.88 -2.76 37.4 -2.76 C 38.92 -2.76 40.16 -1.52 40.16 0 Z M 37.4 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \color[rgb]{0.9492,0.5742,0.1133}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 21.65 0 M 24.41 0 C 24.41 1.52 23.18 2.76 21.65 2.76 C 20.13 2.76 18.9 1.52 18.9 0 C 18.9 -1.52 20.13 -2.76 21.65 -2.76 C 23.18 -2.76 24.41 -1.52 24.41 0 Z M 21.65 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\frac{3\log\left(x\right)-2\left(-6\log\left(\tau_{3}\right)+3\log(\epsilon)+\pi^{2}-6\right)}{24\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}}\,.

(2.153)

When adding all the diagrams, we find that the divergences cancel as well as the
\log\tau_{3}
terms.
The result is in perfect agreement with (
2.146
); the functions appearing in the single integrals and the integration complexity however increase in this
R
-symmetry channel, and the combination of non-perturbative techniques with perturbative computations represents an advantage. In the following sections, we will prove how this advantage allows the computation of correlators of half-BPS scalar operators having unspecified scaling dimensions. Beyond providing an explicit check of the validity of the perturbative bootstrap approach, the integrals reported in this section can also be used to compute correlation functions of non-primary operators obtained by acting with differential operators on
\mathcal{O}_{2}(x_{1})
.

CFT data.

The results above can be used for determining the CFT data at next-to-leading order.
Notice that the expressions (
2.144
) and (
2.146
) are entirely
rational
, i.e., no transcendental functions are appearing at this order.
This is surprising, since we know that the operators exchanged in the OPE generally have anomalous dimensions at one loop, while we have seen in (
2.138
) that operators with even
{\hat{\Delta}}
have a tree-level OPE coefficient.
This observation can be summarized in the following equation:

\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(0)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(0)}\gamma_{\hat{\Delta}}^{(1)}\,\rangle=0\,,

(2.154)

where the brackets
\langle\,\ldots\,\rangle
should be understood in the average sense explained in (
2.111
) and below.
We comment on the absence of logarithmic terms in Section
2.4.2
.

In (
2.138
), we saw that operators with odd tree-level scaling dimension have vanishing OPE coefficients at order
{\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{0}/N)
.
They start to contribute at one loop, and we find the following closed form for their average:

\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\,\rangle_{{\hat{\Delta}}\text{ odd}}=\frac{(-1)^{{\hat{\Delta}}/2}i\,\Gamma({\hat{\Delta}}+2)}{2^{{\hat{\Delta}}+5/2}\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma({\hat{\Delta}}+3/2)}H_{{\hat{\Delta}}+1}\,,

(2.155)

with
H_{n}
the harmonic numbers defined through

H_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}\,.

(2.156)

The lowest operator described by (
2.155
) corresponds to
\phi^{6}
and is not degenerate.
Its corresponding OPE coefficient is given by

b_{2{\phi^{6}}}^{(1)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}\phi^{6}}^{(1)}=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\pi}\,.

(2.157)

Long operators with an even tree-level scaling dimension receive corrections to their tree-level value given in (
2.138
).
It is difficult to find an analytic closed form, however, they can be expressed in the form of a recursion relation:

\displaystyle\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\,\rangle_{{\hat{\Delta}}\text{ even}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(1)_{{\hat{\Delta}}/2}}{12\sqrt{2}(5/2)_{{\hat{\Delta}}/2}}\biggl(1-\frac{4}{\pi}\frac{({\hat{\Delta}}+1)({\hat{\Delta}}+3)}{{\hat{\Delta}}^{2}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\phantom{=\ }-\frac{{\hat{\Delta}}/2+1}{\Gamma(-{\hat{\Delta}}/2)}\sum_{h=2\text{ step }2}^{{\hat{\Delta}}-2}\frac{i^{h}\Gamma(h+5/2)\Gamma((h-{\hat{\Delta}})/2)}{\Gamma^{2}(h/2+1)\Gamma((h+{\hat{\Delta}}+5)/2)}\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\,\rangle\,.

(2.158)

Although this is not a closed form, note that recursion relations are extremely efficient, even for high
{\hat{\Delta}}
.
In the formula above, we used the Pochhammer symbols, defined through

(a)_{n}=\frac{a!}{(a-n)!}\,.

(2.159)

Strong coupling

AdS
5
AdS
2
\partial
AdS
5
\partial
AdS
2
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}

Figure 2.6
:
Illustration of the Witten diagrams for the bulk-defect-defect correlators at strong coupling.
As labelled on the figure, the inside of the circle represents the AdS
5
spacetime, while its boundary corresponds to the dual CFT
4
(in this case,
{\mathcal{N}}=4
sYM).
The bold red line represents the string worldsheet that is dual to the Wilson line.
The worldsheet and the boundary CFT coincide on a line, which is the Maldacena-Wilson line (
2.48
).
The bulk operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}
lives in the CFT
4
, while the operators
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}}
and
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}}
are representations of the CFT
1
dual to the AdS
2
surface.
Note that the fact that the worldsheet and the CFT
4
coincide at two points only in the figure is an artefact of the representation.

We now consider the bulk-defect-defect correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
in the strong-coupling regime, providing the leading and next-to-leading orders with the corresponding CFT data
19
19

19

After this thesis was reviewed by the referees, the strong coupling calculation has been updated by the authors of
[
30
]
– independently from the content of the referee report. For this reason, we refer the reader interested in the strong coupling part of the calculation to the updated version of reference
[
30
]
available on arXiv.
. At next-to-next-to-leading order, we determine through an Ansatz the transcendental part of the correlator, and comment on the fact that the rational part is constrained by the sum rules (
2.131
) arising from locality. We study the strong-coupling regime through a perturbative expansion at large
N
of the form

{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}(\zeta;x)=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{N}\biggl({\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(0)}(\zeta;x)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(1)}(\zeta;x)+\frac{1}{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(2)}(\zeta;x)+\ldots\biggr)+\ldots\,.

(2.160)

As in (
2.132
), the dots refer to corrections in
1/\sqrt{\lambda}
inside the brackets and in
1/N
outside the brackets.
The relevant Witten diagrams are listed in Table
2.4
, following the conventions of Figure
2.6
. In this section, we will not compute them and we mostly use them as guides for the discussion; the interested reader can refer to
[
400
,
176
]
for an introduction to the topic of Witten diagrams and holography.

Leading order

At leading order, there is only one disconnected Witten diagram, which corresponds to the contribution of the identity operator.
We have seen in Section
2.3.2
that the identity only appears in
F_{2}
(in the form of a constant), and thus we have

F_{2}^{(0)}(x)=-2a_{2}^{(0)}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,,\qquad F_{1}^{(0)}=0\,.

(2.161)

The CFT data for the long operators is easy to extract and it is

\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(0)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(0)}\,\rangle=0\,.

(2.162)

This expression is conservative, as it represents an average over operators having the same classical dimension and does not give information on each operator. Nevertheless, it is easy to convince oneself that individual operators also have vanishing OPE coefficients as at leading order either they do not couple with the bulk operator or with the two defect ones:

b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(0)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(0)}=0\,.

(2.163)

Next-to-leading order

Table 2.4
:
Witten diagrams contributing to the bulk-defect-defect correlators in the strong-coupling limit (on the CFT side).
The first two diagrams are disconnected and correspond to the factorized limit
\langle\,\Delta_{1}\,\rangle\langle\,{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle
, if non-vanishing.
The third one is connected and was considered in
[
301
]
.
In our setup, it describes the pinching limit
\langle\,\Delta_{1}({\hat{\Delta}}_{2}+{\hat{\Delta}}_{3})\,\rangle
.
The fourth diagram is non-trivial and starts contributing at order
{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\lambda}/N)
.
Note that other diagrams that we do not show certainly also contribute to this order.

\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle=

+

+

+

+\ldots

At next-to-leading order, there are two Witten diagrams contributing to the correlator, namely the second and third ones in Table
2.4
.
The first contribution is disconnected and corresponds to
a_{2}^{(1)}
.
20
20

20

Note that the disconnected part can be evaluated at all orders in
\lambda
using the protected CFT data.

The second one is the leading connected term, which consists only of free propagators.
We conclude then that the
R
-symmetry channels are again constant.
Moreover, this diagram corresponds, up to a prefactor, to the leading term of the bulk-defect correlator
\langle\,2\hat{2}\,\rangle
.
This results in

F_{1}^{(1)}(x)=b_{2\hat{2}}\lambda_{\hat{1}\hat{1}\hat{2}}^{(1)}=\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\,.

(2.164)

Meanwhile, the disconnected term gives

F_{2}^{(1)}(x)=\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2}}\,.

(2.165)

To extract the conformal data at this order, we first observe that the spectrum consists of defect operators with
even
scaling dimensions at tree level, in agreement with expectations from the AdS/CFT correspondence
[
304
,
185
]
.
For instance, the operator
\phi^{6}
flows from
{\hat{\Delta}}=1
at weak coupling to a two-particle state with
{\hat{\Delta}}=2
at strong coupling
[
13
]
.
Although the interpretation differs from weak coupling, the CFT data at the next-to-leading order take the same form up to an overall constant:

\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\,\rangle=\frac{(-1)^{{\hat{\Delta}}/2}3\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma({\hat{\Delta}}+2)}{2^{{\hat{\Delta}}+3/2}\Gamma({\hat{\Delta}}+3/2)}\,.

(2.166)

We remark that since the leading order contributions are zero, there is no term involving anomalous dimensions.

Partial results at next-to-next-to-leading order

Figure 2.7
:
Analytic structure of the
\log
term at next-to-next-to-leading order at strong coupling.
Although individual blocks are discontinuous at
x\geq 1
, the resummed term obeys the locality condition of
[
301
]
.

We now discuss partial results for the next-to-next-to-leading order, in which we expect several Witten diagrams to appear.
One example is the fourth diagram of Table
2.4
.
We give here the transcendental part of the
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
correlator, and discuss constraints on the rational part.

We start by formulating an Ansatz based on the expected structure of the correlator.
In the strong-coupling regime, the transcendentality weight typically increases in steps of
1
with powers of
1/\sqrt{\lambda}

[
52
,
185
]
.
Moreover, since we are dealing with a function of a single spacetime cross-ratio, this means that the correlator takes the following form at transcendentality one:

{\mathcal{A}}_{2\hat{1}\hat{1}}^{(2)}(\zeta;x)=r_{0}(\zeta;x)+r_{1}(\zeta;x)\log x\,,

(2.167)

where the functions
r_{j}(\zeta;x)
are rational functions (both at
x\sim 0
and
x\sim 1
) as well as polynomials of degree one in
\zeta
in order to reflect the structure presented in (
2.133
). We can therefore write the functions
r_{j}(\zeta;x)
as

r_{j}(\zeta;x)=s_{i}^{(0)}+\zeta s_{i}^{(1)}\,.

(2.168)

We can study the logarithmic terms by using the fact that degeneracies are not lifted at next-to-leading order in the scaling dimensions.
21
21

21

See, e.g.,
[
185
]
for an explanation and an application in four-point functions of defect operators.

This means that we can extract the anomalous dimensions from the averages, as it is the same for all degenerate operators:

\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\gamma_{\hat{\Delta}}^{(1)}\,\rangle=\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}\,\rangle\gamma_{\hat{\Delta}}^{(1)}\,.

(2.169)

Note that this statement is assuming that the operators
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\hat{\Delta}}}
are single-trace.
This assumption is justified, as if we denote an arbitrary multi-trace operator of dimension
{\hat{\Delta}}
by
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(\mathbf{{\hat{\Delta}}})}
, we can remark that

b_{2\mathbf{{\hat{\Delta}}}}\sim\begin{cases}{\mathcal{O}}(1)\,,&\text{ if }\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(\mathbf{{\hat{\Delta}}})}=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(0|2)}\,,\\
{\mathcal{O}}(1/N^{2})\,,&\text{ otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(2.170)

Thus, at large
N
, we only need to worry about
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(0|2)}
, since three-point functions of defect multi-trace operators are suppressed.
In fact, the coefficients
b_{2\hat{2}}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}\hat{2}}
and
b_{2(\hat{0}|\hat{2})}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}(\hat{0}|\hat{2})}
are seen to scale as
1/N
by noting that

\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}(\hat{0}|\hat{2})}\sim\frac{1}{N}\,.

(2.171)

This scaling is true if the three-point function
\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}(\hat{0}|\hat{2})}
is non-vanishing. We are now in a similar situation to the one described below equation (
2.113
).
In fact, by requiring that
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{(0|2)}
is orthogonal to
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
, we are setting at the same time
\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}(\hat{0}|\hat{2})}=0
, similarly to (
2.113
).
To summarize, only single-trace operators appear on the left-hand side of (
2.169
).
We believe this to be true at all orders in the coupling constant.

The anomalous dimensions for the long operators
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{[0,0],0}^{\hat{\Delta}}}
are known
[
303
,
185
]
and they are given by the quadratic Casimir eigenvalue for singlets of
R
-symmetry and transverse spin:

\gamma_{{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(1)}=-\frac{{\hat{\Delta}}({\hat{\Delta}}+3)}{2}\,.

(2.172)

Plugging these values inside the superblock expansion and comparing to the Ansatz (
2.167
), we find the rational function corresponding to the log terms. The result is

r_{1}(\zeta;x)=\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}(x-\zeta)\,.

(2.173)

We can further constrain the correlator by using the superconformal Ward identity (
2.90
) to remove one of the remaining two rational functions.
From the expression (
2.89
), we get

s_{1}^{(0)}(x)=\frac{21}{8\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{x}-\frac{s_{0}^{(0)}(x)}{x}\,,

(2.174)

and the correlator at next-to-next-to-leading order is now fully fixed up to the rational function
s_{0}^{(0)}(x)
.

Sum rules.

We conclude this section on strong coupling by mentioning that the remaining rational function can in principle be further constrained from consistency conditions.
The open rational function follows the superblock expansion

\displaystyle s_{0}^{(0)}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{4\sqrt{2}}+\biggl(\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(2)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(2)}\,\rangle_{{\hat{\Delta}}^{(0)}=2}-\frac{9}{10\sqrt{2}}\biggr)x

\displaystyle\phantom{=\ }+\biggl(\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(2)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(2)}\,\rangle_{{\hat{\Delta}}^{(0)}=4}+\frac{8}{9}\langle\,b_{2{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(2)}\hat{\lambda}_{\hat{1}\hat{1}{\hat{\Delta}}}^{(2)}\,\rangle_{{\hat{\Delta}}^{(0)}=2}+\frac{457\sqrt{2}}{945}\biggr)x^{2}+\ldots\,.

(2.175)

Strong constraints on these OPE coefficients can be derived by demanding locality, as it was shown in
[
301
]
.
Since we are interested only in the rational terms, and the logarithmic terms are local, the coefficients of a corresponding bosonic block expansion are constrained by the locality sum rules (
2.131
) in the form

c_{m+n}+\sum_{k=0}^{m}c_{k}\,\theta^{1+m}_{n}(2+2k)=0\,,

(2.176)

where
\theta_{n}^{m}(\Delta)
is defined in Equation (3.27) of
[
301
]
.
Here,
m
is a positive integer assumed to be small but unknown.
The sum rules alone are however insufficient for fixing all the coefficients if
m>0
.
Perhaps an input from the Witten diagram side can help to make the sum rules useful for fixing
s_{0}^{(0)}(x)
.

2.4.2
Generalization to arbitrary
\Delta_{1}
,
{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}
,
{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}

In this section, we present the analysis for the general correlator
\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle
at weak coupling up to next-to-leading order. We do not extract the CFT data, although we note that it is in principle possible to do it case by case in the same way as in the sections above (see for example
2.4.1
). We have seen in (
2.134
) and (
2.142
)-(
2.143
) that the correlator
\langle\,2\hat{1}\hat{1}\,\rangle
is fixed up to next-to-leading order by Feynman diagrams that do not contain bulk vertices. As it will be further clarified below, this observation holds for the general case
\langle\,\Delta_{1}{\hat{\Delta}}_{2}{\hat{\Delta}}_{3}\,\rangle
as well.
We are therefore interested in diagrams of the form

\hbox to127.19pt{\vbox to81.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.14322pt\lower-22.93538pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -47.24 0 L 125.98 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 59.06 C 18.14 38.45 6.96 25.99 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
